Rose Petals Vol. 2 (2012) — Quiz Cheat Sheet

All twelve monthly editions of 2012, from January's “Arati” to December's “The Roar of the Sea – Memory and Awareness”. Cheat-sheet first, then the full question bank — every answer gives you the exact chapter, month, printed page and Guruji's own words, so you can go and read the passage yourself.

96 questions · 70 key teachings · 44 analogies · 66 one-liners · 15 pictures

What this covers — and what it does not

Scope: Rose Petals Vol. 2 only — the 12 monthly editions of 2012 (First Edition, Guru Purnima 2013, Saipatham Publications). Nothing has been imported from any other volume.

How to use it: read a chapter's cheat-sheet, then test yourself on its questions. Tap an option to answer — a right answer scores a point; a wrong one shows you the right answer and the exact passage it came from. Or tap “Show answer” to read the answer without scoring.

Pictures: each monthly edition carries one photo, captioned with place and year only. The book never names who is pictured, so the quiz asks for the caption only — place and year.

Not in Vol. 2 — the cheque-book analogy you asked about

The cheque-book analogy is not in Rose Petals Vol. 2, and it is not in any of the 2012 Telugu editions either — the words “checkbook / check book / cheque book” occur nowhere in Vol. 2 (all 5,450 lines), in any of the 12 monthly English 2012 editions, or in the 2012 Telugu editions. So it is not in the question bank below. It does exist — here is where, and what Guruji actually said.

Where: Rose Petals Vol. 1, Chapter 9, “Concretizing Fulfilment” (September 2011 edition), printed page 69. Guruji was asked: “Guruji, would you explain what you mean by ‘concretization of the abstract’?” It is repeated in Vol. 3, page 151.

What it means: the one lakh rupees already on deposit is the abstract fulfilment — the grace already given. The cheque book is the means of drawing it out into your hand, into lived experience. That drawing-out is concretization. The passage says nothing about Baba beyond this, so no further link is claimed here.

Suppose you know that someone has deposited one lakh rupees in your bank account, but you don't have a cheque book and you can't withdraw it – do you think you are rich or poor? You are rich, but you need a cheque book in order to concretize it, to actually get hold of the rupees. The fact that there's one lakh rupees on deposit is abstract; getting them in your hand by withdrawal – that is concretization.
Rose Petals Vol. 1, Ch. 9 “Concretizing Fulfilment” (September 2011), printed p. 69; repeated in Vol. 3, p. 151.

Also not in this volume: “Hollow Man” / hollowness / shallowness — that is the February 2013 edition (Vol. 3). The words “hollow” and “shallow” do not occur in Vol. 2 at all.

Cheat-sheet — chapter by chapter

Tap a chapter to open it. Key teachings carry Guruji's words verbatim, with the printed book page.

Arati January 2012 · book pp. 3–14
6 teachings · 4 analogies · 5 one-liners

Arati is not a ritual obligation but an expression of love. Guruji reads Dasganu Maharaj's aratis - above all Kakad Arati Padhs X-XI, 'Sai rahama nazara karanaa' - as the voice of a devotee so emptied of ego that he asks only to be the broom (jharu) in Baba's masjid, collecting the dust of devotees' feet. Arati has no inherent value; its value is the way you express it and how it makes you feel. Even silent, happy listening is participating, and the company of co-lovers (fellow lovers of Baba) itself wets a dry mind with love.

Key teachings

Arati has no inherent value; its only value is the expression and the feeling it gives.

It is just an expression, there is no inherent value in it. The only inherent value is the way you express it and how it makes you feel.
p. 8

The real arati stupefies the mind: singing should leave you dumbfounded, forgetting yourself.

And, at least to some extent, when we sing the arati our minds should get stupefied: that is the real arati.
p. 5

Dasganu, an orthodox Brahmin, asked only to be the broom in Baba's masjid, collecting the dust of devotees' feet.

It is enough for me that I collect the dust of your devotees' feet. This is my puja and I'm happy with that.
p. 6

Baba is a companion from beginning to end, not a last-minute rescuer.

Actually, not only 'to the end' or 'at the climax', but 'from beginning to end' - he will be there. Not like the police in a Telugu movie who will come in the last scene, but who won't be of any help, of course!
p. 8

Just listening happily is participating in arati; nobody has to sing.

But just sitting there and happily enjoying listening to it and looking at Baba with respect, that is participating.
p. 9

Being among co-lovers multiplies joy: seeing your own Beloved loved by so many.

When one object of love is loved by so many people, then the whole expression of that love is everywhere and that gives an added enjoyment.
p. 10

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
broom (jharu) in the masjidDasganu's chosen self-image: the broom is untouchable (it touches dirt), yet it alone has the fortune of collecting the dust of Baba's feet and of devotees' feet, then rests untouched in a corner. It pictures egoless devotion - choosing the lowest place.Apane masjida ka jharu Ganu hai [Ganu is your mosque's broom]. (p. 11)
Kabir's dogKabir related to Ram as his dog - 'Main Ram ka kuttaa hun!' - happy with a single biscuit, always going around his master. Guruji holds this up against our ego, which bristles at bowing two or three times to Baba's photo.Kabir said, 'Main Ram ka kuttaa hun!' 'I am Ram's dog!' (p. 11)
greeting cardIf you have no spontaneous expression of love of your own, pick a ready-made one that suits you - as you would choose a birthday card in a shop - and use it. Arati is such a ready-made expression; send your best wishes to Baba.You go to a shop and search through all the different cards there until you find one you like, that you actually connect to. Then you buy that card and send it to your friend, on his birthday, or on Christmas, or on New Year's Day, whatever it is. (p. 9)
dry mind made wetSitting in a group surrounded by others' love removes the mind's dryness: 'that dryness goes and we become wet with the love of our surroundings.'Without our realizing it, sometimes the mind becomes dry and being in a group, surrounded by so many people's love, sitting among them, that dryness goes and we become wet with the love of our surroundings. (p. 10)

One-liners to remember

  1. In Baba's arati we sing, 'Thaka gayi meri rasanaa,' that is, 'I am dumbfounded' [lit. 'my tongue is exhausted']. (p. 5)
  2. So, when people asked me what to do in their satsangs, I said not all the aratis are needed; if you want, you can just sing Sai rahama nazara karanaa. (p. 5)
  3. He was so confident that Baba was a Muslim that he wanted to express it in a Muslim fashion, so he used Islamic wording. (p. 7)
  4. Do it then. Not that one has to sing the arati to Baba or otherwise he won't shower his grace: that is wrong. (p. 9)
  5. So try to do the arati with meaning! (p. 13)
The Aesthetic Experience February 2012 · book pp. 15–24
5 teachings · 5 analogies · 7 one-liners

Beauty is not in artworks outside us but in our own life, mind and body: each person is a piece of art more beautiful than Michelangelo's. Aesthetic experience (rasanubhuti) is feeling one with something and forgetting yourself - as when a tragedy makes you weep with joy. Scriptures are the rishis' ecstatic utterances, art not science; life itself is a novel written by Baba, to be read and enjoyed, not a textbook to be annotated. Paradox is a beautiful element of poetry, and the Sadguru's greatness makes us feel small without belittling us.

Key teachings

Your own life is the artwork; be a Michelangelo of your own experience rather than chasing outside art.

I prefer that instead of going for the works of Michelangelo, everybody tries to be a Michelangelo and experiences their own art.
p. 16

Scriptures are expressions of ecstatic love, not dry science; that which affects the heart is real art.

All the scriptures are expressions of love. Try to see that.
p. 17

Life is a novel written by Baba, not a textbook; read it and enjoy it.

This life is a novel, a beautiful novel! Written by whom? Written by Baba. He is the author. Try to read it and enjoy it - don't make it a textbook!
p. 18

Aesthetic experience is oneness with the object, forgetting yourself.

Aesthetic experience is where you feel one with something, you forget yourself. In Sanskrit it's called rasanubhuti, aesthetics.
p. 19

The guru's greatness makes us feel small, but that does not belittle us - we enjoy it; that is the paradox of the Sadguru relationship.

That enjoyment, the aesthetics of it, is the relationship between us and our Sadguru.
p. 22

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
MichelangeloJust as Michelangelo experienced his own art in creating it, we should experience the art of our own mind and life instead of only admiring works on canvas.Just think how much he must have experienced his own art! Like that, experience your own creations. (p. 16)
novel vs textbookRead the book of life like a good novel - remembering, enjoying, reproducing it easily - not like a textbook you must annotate and reproduce under fear of failing.Then the book of life will be like a good novel, a pleasure to read; otherwise it will be like a textbook. (p. 18)
Rakta Sambandham (Telugu tragedy film)A man saw this tragedy 200 times, going just to cry - because crying there is an aesthetic experience that gives joy. Likewise Shakespeare's tragedies outshine his comedies, not from sadism (a sadist would not cry) but from rasa.I know a person who has seen that movie two hundred times. Going there just to cry! (p. 19)
baby crying 'baaa baaa' / all poetryA poet cannot describe the experience, but tries anyway; the longing behind the words is enough - as a mother understands each different cry of her baby, though there are no words.In fact, all poetry is baaa baaas. The mother understands the longing behind it. (p. 21)
great mountain / feeling smallStanding before a great mountain we enjoy feeling small; so too before a Sadguru (guru means 'big') we feel little yet are not belittled - a paradox we enjoy.He's so big that he makes us feel so little, but that does not belittle us! We enjoy it. It is a paradox again. (p. 22)

One-liners to remember

  1. To me, every one of you is a great piece of art! (p. 16)
  2. That which affects the heart is real art! (p. 17)
  3. The saints and rishis are all artists. Even the divine - God - is also referred to as an artist, a kavi. (p. 17)
  4. Life is short. Don't waste it on those things which are not at all connected to why you are here. Remember your purpose! (p. 18)
  5. A poet is one who tries. (p. 20)
  6. Life is a book, a beautiful poem. Try to experience its beautiful aesthetics. (p. 22)
  7. Everywhere there is beauty, everywhere there is poetry! (p. 23)
Start From Where You Are March 2012 · book pp. 25–32
6 teachings · 2 analogies · 5 one-liners

Start from your actual reality - what you want, what is missing - not from grand impersonal goals like mukti or killing the ego, which are tall talk, delusion and self-hypnotization. Temporary happiness (the bird in the hand) is real; let the path be happy too, and let temporary happiness expand into lasting happiness. Your goal and path must be your own personal ones: your nirvana, not the Buddha's. Guruji points to no goal; he only asks you to know your own direction.

Key teachings

First know what you want; that becomes the reference point to which all life is moulded.

First, know what you want. Then that becomes the reference point to which all our life is moulded.
p. 25

Let the path be happy, not only the goal; temporary happiness expands into lasting happiness - they are not two.

So let the path also be happy, not only the goal! Why should the path to happiness be one of suffering? Let the path to happiness be also happy.
p. 26

'Erasing desires' is a dangerous, ungrounded concept; we have so many desires, and petty things will keep disturbing us.

It may be beautiful to listen to, but 'erasing desires' is another dangerous concept in the idea of lasting happiness because it is not true for us.
p. 27

Start from where you are, from the reality of your own experience, and accept nothing else.

First, know your own reality. Accept your own reality, accept nothing else but your own experience - however profound a concept it may be, or whoever said it.
p. 30

Your goal must be personal: your nirvana, your path - not the Buddha's, Baba's or Ramana's.

But that nirvana should be your nirvana, your personal nirvana, not the Buddha's nirvana, not Sai Baba's nirvana nor Ramana Maharshi's nirvana - it should be your own nirvana!
p. 29

Guruji points to no goal for anyone; he only asks you to know your own direction.

I am not pointing you in any direction. I am asking you to know your own direction, what your goal is, and then go in that direction.
p. 31

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
bird in the hand / two birds in the bushThe small fulfilment we have now is the bird in the hand; promised lasting happiness is the two birds in the bush. Those fixated on the bush lose even the happiness in hand. Both are birds - temporary and lasting happiness are not two separate things.The small fulfilment you are getting now is like having one bird in the hand, and the lasting happiness which the scriptures promise, is like the proverbial 'two birds in the bush'! (p. 26)
beating about the bushChasing a goal you do not really want, you spend your whole life beating about the bush - while even one percent gained from your real starting point is real.Otherwise, all your life you'll be beating about the bush, trying to achieve something you don't really want. (p. 27)

One-liners to remember

  1. Rather, let us start from where we are and let us evolve from there with the reality, whatever it may be, of who and where we are. (p. 27)
  2. Don't leave where you have started from! (p. 29)
  3. Isn't that what he said, 'Make your own path'? (p. 29)
  4. Let your path be your own, let your goal be your own, and let what you get be your own. (p. 29)
  5. Let everything be yours! (p. 31)
In the Orbit of the Magnet April 2012 · book pp. 33–44
6 teachings · 5 analogies · 4 one-liners

The seeker is a piece of iron, the Sadguru a magnet: same material, but the magnet's molecules are ordered and polarized while the iron's pulls conflict and cancel out. Repeated contact with the magnet polarizes the iron without adding or removing a single molecule - that is grace. What transforms the iron is not the magnet but its field, experienced by us as love. Our only effort is to put ourselves in contact, as clothes are put in the sun to dry. Being in the orbit is enough; eventually the iron merges with the sun.

Key teachings

Iron and magnet differ only in order: the magnet's molecules are polarized and aligned; the iron's pulls conflict.

In the magnet all the molecules are in order, properly polarized, and every molecule has a north pole and a south pole. In the piece of iron, the molecules are not polarized - there are no poles and no order, so each molecule's magnetic field is counteracted, or resisted, by another molecule, yet potentially, all the iron molecules are magnets.
p. 33

Contact with the Sadguru polarizes us without adding or losing anything - that is grace.

They will be exactly the same number but their direction will be changed, their attitude will be changed, they'll become properly polarized, harmonized, and they will be aligned and put in order.
p. 34

What transforms the iron is the magnetic field, not the magnet itself - and we experience the field as love.

The real contact is to be in the magnetic field. What transforms a piece of iron into a magnet is not the actual magnet, it's the field.
p. 39

Magnetism is not given by the Sadguru; it is already in the iron - he only awakens it.

Magnetism is not given. It is already there in the piece of iron, so he only awakens it.
p. 37

Baba is our magnet; Ramana called Arunachala a magnetic mountain and stuck to it like iron.

Baba is our magnet. Try to be with him mentally, and if possible, physically, in close proximity - the touch!
p. 38

Being in the orbit of the magnet is enough; the magnet takes care of the rest.

It's enough. Being in the orbit is enough. He'll take care of the rest.
p. 42

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
iron and magnetWe are pieces of iron whose many pulls (money, comfort, family, nirvana) conflict and cancel our force; the Sadguru is the magnet whose ordered, polarized molecules align ours by repeated contact, turning iron into magnet with nothing added or lost.So, in our hearts, we are like those iron molecules. We have so many different pulls with no alignment or order, only different molecules of attraction and desire in conflict with each other. (p. 34)
wet clothes drying in the sunClothes dry only by contact with the sun; our only effort is to put them on the line outside. Likewise the iron's only effort is to be in contact with the magnet, frequently, not once.Our effort is only to put them outside on the line in the sun. And the only effort of the iron is to be in contact with the magnet, frequently in contact, not just once and then saying, 'Yes, I did it, but nothing happened!' (p. 35)
wireless modem / extenderIf you cannot get the signal on the ground floor, you put an extender - another modem - to strengthen it. Likewise, if you cannot get direct contact, do anything that keeps you in the field of the signal.Suppose there is a computer, and a wireless modem is put somewhere on the first floor but you can't get the signal here on the ground floor, what do you do? You put an extender here, another modem, which makes the signal stronger, that's all. So... do anything that keeps you in the field of the signal. (p. 39)
race to grace (guru-G)Complete fulfilment is our goal and we race towards it; the 'G'-factor - the guru - is what makes the race the grace.Complete fulfilment is our goal and we are all racing towards that goal! And when we get it, it becomes grace. That 'G'-factor is the guru, that which makes the race the grace - guru-G! (p. 43)
magnet drawing iron (statically dynamic)The iron seems active as it moves to the magnet, but it is the static magnet that draws it - the magnet is statically dynamic; likewise the Sadguru draws the seeker.Actually, it is the magnet which draws, which is active, although it seems as if it is the piece of iron that is active because it's moving towards the magnet and the magnet is static, not doing anything. But the magnet is moving it. So it is statically dynamic. (p. 41)

One-liners to remember

  1. The attraction that a piece of iron has towards the magnet is experienced as love. The piece of iron loves the magnet. (p. 41)
  2. If the dissatisfaction is real you'll draw nearer and nearer in a closer and closer orbit. Eventually you'll merge with the sun and become one with it. (p. 42)
  3. On its own the piece of iron can't do anything, so it needs a magnet. That magnet is called a Sadguru. (p. 43)
  4. He said that his guru always loved and protected him wherever he was. (p. 41)
Niyama May 2012 · book pp. 45–56
6 teachings · 2 analogies · 6 one-liners

Niyama is a regulation - consistency in a good action - whose value is that it does not let your own mind cheat you of your fulfilment. The mind begins with a noble excuse (meditate at home instead of satsang) and slides within days to sleep, movies and chitchat. Guruji's own niyama: in 1971 his Master told him to take charge of the Thursday satsang at Vidyanagar, and for six years he never missed once - walking 6 km through floods and cyclones, with 104-105 fever, and punishing the mind's tricks the next day (cold bath at 2:45 a.m., walking twice when he missed a bus). Strength comes from the desire to reach the goal; the niyama was not from family conditioning but experienced as helpful, and became spontaneous.

Key teachings

Niyama is consistency in a good action; it stops the mind cheating you of your fulfilment.

It doesn't allow you to be cheated by your own mind, because it keeps you to the goal, to the purpose, so you are more oriented towards your goal.
p. 45

The mind's trick starts nobly - 'I want to sit at home in meditation' - then slides to sleep, movie, chitchat, and satsang is gone.

First it starts like that. The second day our resolve loses some more strength. It's a trick of the mind to avoid going to satsang.
p. 46

Niyama means what you are supposed to do - a regulation, a code of conduct (yogasastras).

Niyama means - what you are supposed to do, a regulation.
p. 46

Guruji kept the Thursday satsang niyama for six years without missing once, whatever the weather or fever.

That is a niyama: I didn't miss doing it, not even once. If floods were expected, I used to go earlier, at five o'clock, and take a raft and then reach Vidyanagar.
p. 48

Strength to hold a niyama comes from the desire to reach the goal.

I don't know where from, but I think that it is from our desire to reach the goal. Only that can give the strength.
p. 55

Guruji judges people by what they do, not what they say.

That's why I never believe what people say, I always take what they do.
p. 55

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
the mind's slippery slope (satsang to chitchat)Day 1 the mind says meditate at home instead of satsang; day 2 resolve weakens; day 3 sleep; day 4 a movie; day 5 chitchat - 'Where is satsang? It's gone!' The trick begins with something noble so the mind feels justified.Then the next day the person sleeps. The third day he goes to a movie. The fourth day he goes and chitchats. Where is satsang? It's gone! (p. 46)
punishing the mind (cold bath / double walk)When the mind balked at a 3 a.m. cold bath, Guruji got up at 2:45 a.m. with colder pot-water the next day; when it leaned on a bus, he walked twice from Vidyanagar to Kota - so the mind stopped daring even to think the thought.Next day what I used to do was, I put some water in an earthen storage pot and kept it there [which made it very cold], and then I got up even earlier, at 2.45 a.m. and used that water - that cold, freezing water - for my bath. Next time the mind didn't even dare to think that thought! (p. 49)

One-liners to remember

  1. In 1971, when I first met my Master, he said, 'Take charge of the satsang!' (p. 48)
  2. 'What will happen? I will die, that's all! Will anything more happen? No. Go!' (p. 48)
  3. Self-trickery - at least I can stop that. (p. 51)
  4. I simply thought, 'My guru has given me a responsibility.' I took it as a blessing and then I did it, that's all. (p. 52)
  5. If it is not sufficient, that is his fault, it is not my fault. (p. 52)
  6. I didn't try to keep it up. It was not keeping up something. It happened spontaneously. It became a part of the whole thing. (p. 54)
Light on Anger June 2012 · book pp. 57–66
5 teachings · 3 analogies · 6 one-liners

Ego is what we think we are in the eyes of others - more precisely, what we think others think of us. Anger springs when that image is disturbed, and has three causes: (1) remembering or anticipating something that affects our image (imaginary), (2) misunderstanding, lack of clarity, (3) pain or discomfort (real). The blind man who steps on your toe shows it is not pain but the expectation of politeness that hurts; a child jumping on your chest causes pain but no anger, because there is love. The remedy is clarity for the first two causes and love for the third; ultimately only love kindled by a Sadguru lets us see ignorance instead of evil.

Key teachings

Ego is the image of what we think we are in the eyes of others - what we think others think of us.

Normally in our experience, ego is what we think we are in the eyes of others; it's not an exact definition, but it gives us some idea.
p. 57

Anger has three causes: image-disturbing memory/anticipation, misunderstanding, and pain/discomfort.

Grossly speaking, these are the three reasons for anger. If you think of any of the instances when you get angry, it will be one of these three.
p. 58

Our anger proves our self-image is false: we expect respect and love that is not forthcoming.

Our anger shows that the image we have of ourselves, of what others think of us, is false.
p. 61

Understanding the mechanism behind anger makes it cease, then slowly vanish.

If we can understand the mechanism behind our anger, it will cease. It may come back, but again if we can understand it, it will slowly vanish.
p. 61

Only love kindled by a Sadguru lets us see ignorance, not evil - as Jesus forgave from the cross and Baba forgave like a parent.

Only by having the love kindled in your heart by a Sadguru. Once you have the love in you then you don't see evil; you can see only ignorance.
p. 64

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
blind man steps on your toe (bus/train)A man steps on your toe and you shout - then you learn he is blind, and anger instantly becomes apology and compassion, though the pain is unchanged. So it was not pain but the expectation of politeness that hurt.What hurt you was not the physical pain. If that was the reason you would've still been angry because the pain remained, your toe still hurt. So it was not the pain that triggered anger. (p. 59)
child jumping on your chestA child standing and jumping on your chest is definitely painful, yet you say 'Jump more!' - because there is love. Pain alone is not a reason for anger.When you play with your child and she stands on your chest and starts jumping up and down, it's painful. It's definitely painful, but you say, 'Oh, my sweet, come on! Jump more! Jump more!' (p. 60)
cutting onions (tears are not crying)Bodily reactions are not the emotion itself: shedding tears is not crying, just as cutting onions brings tears. Don't bother about physical reactions; attend to the basic emotion.Just shedding tears is not crying. It is the suffering, the sorrow that you feel in your heart that is the actual sorrow. Even if you cut onions you'll get tears in your eyes. (p. 62)

One-liners to remember

  1. Because what we think usually depends on what others think of us. In fact, not what others think, but rather what we think others think of us! (p. 58)
  2. Why should we expect love and respect from others and why should we get angry with them? Why do we beg for affection, concern, love and all these things from other people? (p. 61)
  3. What you expect is that I should react to you with love, with concern, with understanding. (p. 63)
  4. What did Jesus say? Even when he was being crucified, nailed to the cross, what was it he said? 'Father, they know not what they do. Forgive them.' (p. 64)
  5. He said, 'If a child passes a stool on your leg, will you hit her? Will you cut off your leg?' No, because a child doesn't know what she is doing. (p. 64)
  6. By understanding, by concern, by sympathizing with the other person and understanding their situation. With that, the anger won't stay. (p. 64)
The Chemistry of Love July 2012 · book pp. 67–80
6 teachings · 5 analogies · 8 one-liners

Love - not bhakti as a doctrine, but love itself - is the source, the key and the driving force: Ramana left home out of love for Arunachala, Buddha out of love for solving life's mystery. Right effort is simply an expression of love; effort does not produce the result - grace does, and grace depends on need, not effort. The ego is not to be killed but merges into love like fuel burnt in a flame (the Sadguru's form is the wick). Even 10% love is good, like one iddly to a hungry man. Love grows only in a congenial environment, which you must create - as Himalayan farmers terrace slopes to grow rice. The path is happiness from start to finish.

Key teachings

Love is the key and the driving force of every genuine seeking.

So love is the driving force. It happens in different ways in different people.
p. 68

Ramana left home out of love for Arunachala, not renunciation, maya or a wish for mukti.

What actually drove him from Madurai to Arunachala was simply his love of Arunachala, that's all.
p. 68

Right effort is an expression of love; loving the method more than its object puts the cart before the horse.

The correct effort is always experienced as an expression of our love.
p. 69

The ego is not killed or destroyed; in love it merges, is burnt up as fuel, and loses its identity.

Instead, what I say is, when you experience love, the ego gradually merges into it and becomes one with that love. It gets totally merged and absorbed in it, and loses its identity and form.
p. 71

Effort does not bring the result; grace does - and grace depends on need, not effort.

Our effort - our so-called effort - is an expression of our need, our love, and we can't help it, we have to do it. And by expressing and expressing our need more and more, our love becomes more, it becomes stronger.
p. 74

There is no technique to make love grow; give it a congenial environment and it grows on its own.

There is no method or technique to make love grow. The only thing is, when you realize that you have love and you have a need for love, and you want to realize that love, then try to create or be in an environment where that love is widely cultivated. Then it will grow on its own.
p. 76

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
ego as fuel, oil and wickThe ego is the fuel that supports the flame of love, like oil beneath a lamp-flame: it supports the fire, then disappears into it. The Sadguru's form is the wick (not wicked!). In love alone, by losing one gains.It is in the ego that love is kindled. And when the love grows, the ego is burnt up in the fire of love - it is no more there. It's just like fuel. Fuel is the support for fire, but after some time it disappears, it is no more there; it is present in a different way. (p. 72)
one iddly to a hungry man (10% love)Even 10% love is good because it is love: a hungry man does not refuse a plate of iddlis for want of a full meal. Love grows by degrees until no more is needed.Even if it's not 100%, 10% love is also good, because it is love. If you are hungry and you don't get a full meal, will you refuse just a plate of iddlis? (p. 75)
plant and environment / terracing Himalayan slopesYou cannot make a plant grow by pulling it straight; you give water, fertilizer and a proper environment. Himalayan farmers terrace rocky slopes to grow rice where rice should not grow - create your own environment for love.When a congenial environment is provided, the plant grows, that's all. What can you do to make a plant grow? Nothing. You can only give it the support of water, fertilizer, and the proper environment. (p. 76)
computer hard disk frozen (capacity)Capacity is the strength to love, receive and hold on to love undisturbed. Like a frozen computer with an empty hard disk, you spend hours with Disk Warrior, Norton and TechTool to recover what you love - love itself gives that capacity.If your computer freezes and your hard disk is empty, how do you feel? You spend hours and hours, you try Disk Warrior, Norton, TechTool, repairing permissions and connecting this and that - so many things you do, constantly, trying to bring that back. (p. 78)
ex-pressing love / knots of the heartLove is pressed in the heart by fears, patterns and desires; it must be ex-pressed, brought out - removing what Vedanta calls the granthis, the knots of the hridaya granthi.Because that love has been pressed in your heart due to so many fears, patterns, wants and desires, you have to ex-press it, you have to bring it out from your heart. This is removing what the Vedantins call the granthis or 'knots' of the heart. (p. 70)

One-liners to remember

  1. If you go to a Sadguru like Baba he will take care of it, the whole process and how it happens. The only thing we have to do is to keep ourselves open, that's all. (p. 67)
  2. It is love, whether of seeking or of the goal. (p. 68)
  3. We give more stress to the method than to the object of it, and end up loving the method more than the object of our love. (p. 69)
  4. What gives you the strength to face it is love. (p. 70)
  5. The form of the Sadguru acts like the wick, not wicked, but wick, though to you he may sometimes seem wicked! (p. 72)
  6. You love what you need, that's all. So, first, you should know what you need. (p. 73)
  7. Grace is not dependent on your effort, but on your need. (p. 74)
  8. We start with happiness, we walk the path in happiness, and we end in happiness - then we are treading the right path. (p. 75)
Clarity and Fulfilment August 2012 · book pp. 81–92
6 teachings · 3 analogies · 5 one-liners

Guruji's path, if it must be named, is a path of clarity and fulfilment - not devotion, jnana or karma yoga, words he does not use. Clarity is needed for everything, even to be clear about Self-realization; and clarity must be earned, not given. Without clarity our desires pull in different directions and we live on the horns of a dilemma; with clarity (e.g. 80% worldly, 20% spiritual) every part of life can be enjoyed without conflict, as iron molecules aligned by rubbing on a magnet. Love makes focus maintenance-free. Three things to remember: have a goal and remember why you came; guard against dilution and diversion; keep contact with what triggers love. Life is short - get clarity.

Key teachings

Guruji's path is a path of clarity and fulfilment - that is the name he gave when asked.

So finally, I said, 'Even though it is not a path, just tell them, Guruji's path is a path of clarity and fulfilment.' Because that's what is important: clarity! For everything, clarity!
p. 81

Clarity must be earned, not waited for as a gift.

You earn your clarity - I'm asking you to earn it. I'm not saying it will be given, but that you must earn it.
p. 82

Clarity about priorities (80% worldly, 20% spiritual) ends inner conflict and lets both be enjoyed.

If you have that clarity, then when you spend twenty percent of your time in spiritual pursuits, you will experience it happily, that twenty percent, and you will also experience the other eighty percent happily.
p. 83

Choose the goal that triggers the most love and gives the most happiness - whatever it is.

How to choose? That I will say: the one that gives you the most happiness, that triggers the most love in your heart. Choose that goal, whatever it is.
p. 86

When love maintains focus, focus is maintenance-free.

There is no need to maintain a focus: when love maintains it, the focus is 'maintenance free'.
p. 87

The three things to remember: a goal, guarding it from diversion, and keeping contact with what triggers love.

First, you should have a goal, an objective. You should know what you want and what you are striving for. Then you should remember your objective, if at all you have one.
p. 89

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
iron and magnet (clarity recapitulation)Clarity is the same principle as the magnet chapter: iron and magnet have the same molecules; the magnet's are aligned and its entropy gone. Remembering Baba again and again is rubbing on the magnet until you are harmonized.What is the difference between a piece of iron and a magnet? The number of molecules is exactly the same in both. What distinguishes them is that in the magnet, each molecule is aligned, polarized, and harmonized with the others; its entropy has gone, it has become a magnet. (p. 83)
examination / degreeIf you say you want to pass an examination but go to movies and loiter on the beach, Guruji says: study hard, sit in your room and study hard. If you will not study, at least be clear you do not want the degree.You say, 'I want to pass this examination. Oh, I want to very much, I want this degree!' You come to me and talk like this. What can I tell you? I say, 'Yes, if you really want this, then study hard!' (p. 85)
goalkeeperOnce you set your own goal, you must act like a good goalkeeper, keeping other balls from coming in and hitting the goal.Then, just like a good goalkeeper, you have to keep other balls from coming in and hitting the goal. (p. 85)

One-liners to remember

  1. Fulfilment and clarity go together - the path is a path of clarity and fulfilment. (p. 82)
  2. I never advise about goals. I never say that this is the best goal or that is the second-best goal. The choice of goal is yours, you have to choose your own goal. (p. 86)
  3. To blame is to 'be lame'. (p. 88)
  4. First you free them, then you'll be free from them, at least some kind of liberty and freedom. (p. 88)
  5. We do not know where we will be tomorrow, yet we think and plan as though we were permanent and immortal, as though we will live to the end of creation! We plan, we store, we accumulate - is all this needed? Get clarity! (p. 90)
Sri Poondi Swami: The Transmitter Who Never Fails September 2012 · book pp. 93–106
6 teachings · 3 analogies · 5 one-liners

Poondi Swami (first seen c.1935 at Kalasapakkam; mahasamadhi 1978) lived as a madman - filthy, never bathing, pockets stuffed with cigarette stubs, never asking for anything, fed like a child - yet was a perfect saint in ajagarabhava (python-state): he walked for a month without stopping, then sat 18 years on a porch at Poondi. The Kanchi Shankaracharya called him the transmitter to our light bulbs: bulbs may go out, the transmitter never fails. In 1974 Guruji spent about a month in his presence and was transformed without a word spoken; Poondi Swami was so transparent that through him Guruji saw only Sai Baba. His role, like Guruji's own, is that of a spoon: Baba makes and gives the soup.

Key teachings

The Kanchi Shankaracharya's verdict: we are bulbs, Poondi Swami is the transmitter - and a transmitter never fails.

If we are all bulbs, Sri Poondi Swami is the transmitter. These bulbs do not light up until the transmitter is switched on.
p. 96

In Poondi Swami's presence Guruji was transformed, though Swami said nothing.

He didn't say anything. But in his presence I experienced something which transformed my whole life.
p. 97

Poondi Swami was so transparent that through him Guruji saw only Sai Baba.

And he was so transparent that through him I saw Sai Baba only. That is why I always say that it is Sai Baba who gave the experience.
p. 98

Poondi Swami gave whatever anyone needed - health, spiritual experience, fulfilment, children - with no restriction to 'the mukti department'.

His love was expressed by giving whatever the people who asked him needed. If someone asked for health, he gave it; if someone came for spiritual experience, he gave it; if it was for total fulfilment, he gave it; and if it was for children, he gave it.
p. 99

Guruji's role - and Poondi Swami's - is only that of a spoon; Baba makes and gives the soup.

That is why I always say, I don't do anything to you, use me only as a spoon. I am a spoon, use me properly. The one who gives is Baba.
p. 103

Baba's description of his own guru tallies exactly with Poondi Swami: simply sitting, an embodiment of love, unable even to attend to nature's calls.

My guru was just simply sitting, and he was an embodiment of love. He was not in a position to take care of his own calls of nature even, and I used to serve him, cleaning his body and feeding him.
p. 100

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
transmitter and light bulbsWe are bulbs; Poondi Swami is the transmitter. Bulbs go out or are switched off, but the transmitter always works and never fails - and it has no bulb of its own to show it is working; you know it only by the bulbs lighting up.Sometimes we switch off the lights or a bulb goes out, but the transmitter is still there. In the transmitter, there is no bulb to show it is the transmitter - that's the problem! Whether the transmitter is on or not you do not know by looking at the transmitter. Looking at the bulb you can say, 'Ah, yes, the transmitter is working.' (p. 96)
mirror (Poondi Swami's transparency)Poondi Swami was like a mirror: a fearful photographer saw fear on his face; a friendly man saw friendliness. Whatever he expressed was the visitor's expression, not his own - he himself had no expression at all.He was just like a mirror. For example, if a photographer had fear and approached him timidly, then that same expression would come on Swami's face, simply that. He was always like a mirror. (p. 98)
spoon and soupThe guru is a spoon: you take soup with a spoon, but the spoon neither made nor gave the soup. Bite the spoon thinking it gives, and you will lose your teeth.You take soup with a spoon: but who made the soup? Who gave you the soup? And what is the soup? The spoon is only a means, and you should know how to handle it properly. If you think the spoon is giving and then try to bite into it, you'll lose your teeth, that's all! (p. 103)

One-liners to remember

  1. A transmitter never fails. He is the transmitter who never fails. (p. 96)
  2. In fact, I don't have any words for that experience. I haven't found any words in all the sastras. (p. 98)
  3. It was not my choice to go to Poondi. (p. 100)
  4. He is absolutely the symbol of someone who doesn't require anything. He was totally free of all needs, including even his physical needs. (p. 101)
  5. If you are really interested to know what it is, try to catch hold of the fringe and go ahead, continue, go ahead! Plunge, and you will experience it. (p. 103)
Holy Places October 2012 · book pp. 107–118
6 teachings · 6 analogies · 5 one-liners

A place is holy because of a Sadguru's presence: Shirdi is holy because it is Baba's place - no Ganges, no Himalayas, nothing else. We use that presence mostly unaware, like sleeping on a mattress; the cleaner our mind, the more sensitive we are to it (like spotless white clothes fearing dirt, unlike a bus cleaner's filthy clothes). Holy places amplify: sadhana there gives tenfold (Baba: one rupee becomes ten), but wrongdoing also multiplies a hundredfold - a razor's edge. Shirdi and Poondi are the best stoves to boil the milk of the mind; Baba's grace is the sun drying a wet cloth - techniques and niyamas only make the line to hang it on. Tiruvannamalai is the laundry where Guruji washes you; Poondi is the line where you dry.

Key teachings

A place is holy because of a Sadguru's presence - that alone makes Shirdi holy.

The presence of a Sadguru makes a place holy. A real holy place is one in the vicinity of a Sadguru. Why is Shirdi a holy place? Because it is Baba's place.
p. 107

Everything is holy in a real sense; a saint's presence makes holiness manifest more at some places.

In a real sense everything is holy; in fact, there is nothing unholy here. But because of a saint's presence the holiness manifests more at some places than others and these are called 'holy places'.
p. 110

Sadhana in a holy place gives tenfold benefit - but wrongdoing also multiplies; it is a razor's edge.

The great saints said that sadhana done in a holy place, in the presence of great Sadgurus like Ramana Maharshi, gives a tenfold benefit for any effort made. This is what Baba meant when he said: 'If you give me one rupee, I'll give you ten rupees!'
p. 111

Baba's grace is the sun drying a wet cloth; techniques only make the line to hang it on.

Whatever else comes in here - satsangs, niyamas, the so-called spiritual principles or 'commandments' - all these things come under the heading of making the line to hang your cloth in the sun. But what makes it dry is not the line, but the sun.
p. 114

Stay on the line: in Poondi, just be there 'blindly' and stay - that is enough.

And while you are getting dry, what is it that you have to do? Stay on the line, that's all. That is enough. That's why I say, be there 'blindly' in a sense, and just stay on the line.
p. 115

Help is the same whether near or far; near, you experience it more intensely - the sun always shines.

You may experience it to a greater or lesser degree, but I'm talking about giving: my giving is always the same. Like that, the sun is always shining, and the cloth experiences that shining.
p. 117

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
mattressYou are not aware of a mattress while sleeping, yet you use it and benefit - and would miss it if absent. Likewise we use the Sadguru's presence without awareness; sensitivity to its effects is awareness of it.When you sleep on a mattress, are you aware of the presence of the mattress? After getting up you may say, 'Oh, I had a good sleep, this mattress is very good!' But during sleep were you aware of it? Or the presence of it? No, but you were using the presence of it and its absence wouldn't give you the same effect. (p. 108)
bus cleaner's clothesA bus cleaner's oil-filthy clothes do not notice dirt, while spotless white clothes fear even a clean floor. The cleaner the mind, the more it senses the effect of a holy presence the moment it enters it.Some minds are like the cleaner's clothes: wherever they go, even into dirt, they don't notice the dirt at all. But, if you're wearing spotless white clothes that are very, very clean, you'll be so sensitive to dirt that you'll be afraid of sitting, even on a clean floor. (p. 108)
hospital to super-speciality hospitalAs more saints come to a holy place, more channels open: more people can connect. Arunachala grew from a place for certain seekers into a super-speciality spiritual centre, like a hospital that can treat any illness.A hospital becomes a super-speciality hospital, that's all. Before, only those having certain kinds of diseases could be helped. But when a super-speciality hospital comes, any kind of illness can be treated. In the same way, over time some holy places develop into super-speciality spiritual centres, like Arunachala. (p. 110)
milk boiling on the stoveThe mind is milk; Shirdi and Poondi are the best stoves Guruji found. The milk must be ON the stove, not near it - then it boils, overflows and saturates the mind as fulfilment. Left off the stove it curdles and even dogs will not take it.It's as though your mind were like the milk from a cow and you put it on the stove to boil, and the best stoves I have found are in Shirdi and Poondi. To boil milk, you have to keep it on the stove. Not near the stove or by the side of the stove, but on the stove! (p. 112)
wet cloth drying in the sunThe cloth (us) does nothing to dry itself; the sun (Baba's grace) dries it. Our only job is to stay in the sun - but the cloth keeps wandering into the shade or back into the bucket.It's as though you were a wet cloth in the sun, and Baba's grace is like the heat of the sun: all you have to do to become dry is to keep your cloth in the sun. The wetness - our so-called ignorance - will be dried away by the sun, there is nothing the cloth has to do. (p. 113)
washerman's laundry (Tiruvannamalai / Poondi)Guruji is a washerman: in Tiruvannamalai is the laundry room where he washes you with detergents and scrubbing; in Poondi is the line where you hang in the sun to dry. Washing happens in satsang here; drying happens there.Just think of me as a washerman in charge of a laundry here in Tiruvannamalai - here is the laundry room, there are taps, detergents, everything - and I wash you. Afterwards, there is a line in Poondi where I hang you out to dry. (p. 115)

One-liners to remember

  1. Any place is holy because of the presence of a Sadguru. (p. 107)
  2. If you do a good thing the result is a hundredfold, but if you do a wrong thing, the karma will also be a hundredfold. Because that is the law there: whatever you do, the result will be a hundredfold. Presence doesn't discriminate. (p. 111)
  3. The only thing it has to do is: just keep itself in the sun, the drying happens naturally. (p. 113)
  4. It is only the sun that dries. The more you keep a cloth in the sun, the sooner it dries. And the sun is always ready to make it dry. (p. 114)
  5. If you feel it's so easy to do that 'enough', do it! (p. 116)
The Longest Longing November 2012 · book pp. 119–130
6 teachings · 3 analogies · 5 one-liners

Guruji's favourite parable: a sadhu doing tapas under a tree asks Narada to ask Vishnu when he will get darshan. Vishnu answers: after as many births as there are leaves on the tree. Told this, the sadhu dances with joy - 'He's going to give me darshan!' - and his ecstasy burns up the karma of waiting in a moment, so Vishnu appears at once. 'How long?' is a meaningless question: how long is as long as your longing. Even after fulfilment, saints express longing - a joyous longing, union expressed in separation, because union itself cannot be described in words. So call, call, call - Guruji says he would sit 8 to 10 hours a day calling - and Baba will not leave anyone empty-handed.

Key teachings

The mere promise of darshan, received with ecstatic joy, burnt up the karma of thousands of births in a moment.

But, when you told him I would give him darshan even after so many births, the mere promise of my darshan made him so happy that his ecstasy burnt up the karma of those years of waiting in a moment and he was ready.
p. 122

'How long?' is meaningless: how long is as long as your longing - your longest longing.

How much longing you have, is how long. So how long means how long, that is the answer. It is as long as your longing, your long-est longing.
p. 123

The sadhu's story is worth volumes of sastras; it is the essence of the sastras.

It is a beautiful story. It tells hundreds, even millions of things. It is worth volumes of sastras to me, that one story.
p. 123

Saints' longing after fulfilment is joyous longing - union expressed in separation, because union cannot be described.

Yes, there is. But it is not just longing, there is something else in it - there is joy, it is a joyous longing. It is the experience of union expressed in separation.
p. 124

Call, call, call - longing must be expressed as calling, for hours daily.

If you want his presence and you are really honest, earnest, and sincere - call! That is the only way. Call, call, call! Do it! Then you'll get it.
p. 128

Baba will not leave anybody empty-handed.

Baba won't leave anybody, I tell you. Nobody will go empty-handed!
p. 128

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
sadhu, Narada and Vishnu (leaves on the tree)A sadhu under a tree is told darshan comes only after as many births as leaves on the tree; instead of despairing he dances with joy at the promise itself, and that joy burns the waiting-karma instantly. Positive attitude, not the timetable, is the key to darshan. (Compare the sari in the box, Satcharita Ch. 20.)As he said this Narada thought, 'My god, this man will have a heart attack when he hears this!' But to his great surprise, when the man heard it he started dancing with joy, shouting in ecstasy, 'I'm so happy! Lord Vishnu has promised me darshan! He's going to give me darshan!' (p. 121)
starving three days / the mealTo say how much you enjoy a meal after three days without food, you describe the meals you missed, not the one you are eating. Likewise saints describe union by describing separation and longing - the suffering of absence measures the joy of union.It is like explaining how much you are starving when you haven't had a meal in three days. When you eat food again, the first thing that you say is, 'My God, for three days I haven't had a good meal!' (p. 125)
drowning man gasping for breathLonging should be like drowning - gasping 'Haaah! haah! haah!' for one breath. Only then do you know the value of a single breath; then every breath becomes fulfilment, inhaling and exhaling fulfilment. (From Kakad Arati Padh XIII: 'Mi budato bhavabhaya dohi uddhara' - I am drowning in the ocean of worldly desires.)When you are drowning, how do you gasp for breath? That kind of gasping should be there - 'Haaah! haah! haah!' Then you will get the help, Baba will pull you out. Then you'll know the real value of just one breath. (p. 127)

One-liners to remember

  1. See! Again, this is the real prototype of what is said in the Ishavasya Upanishad, and the story [in the Sri Sai Satcharita, Ch. 20] about Dasganu's maid and the sari in the box. (p. 123)
  2. The whole path should be happy, that is what I am saying. If anybody just frets and fumes, they are not on the right path. (p. 125)
  3. So the experience of union cannot be described in words. Therefore the saints don't make the mistake of trying to describe it in words of union. Instead, they use terms of separation and longing. (p. 126)
  4. If I were in your place, I would sit a minimum daily of 8 to 10 hours, calling and calling and calling. (p. 128)
  5. Then every breath becomes so valuable and so blissful and so happy and so fulfilled that we begin to breathe fulfilment - we inhale and exhale fulfilment - we'll become like that. (p. 127)
The Roar of the Sea - Memory and Awareness December 2012 · book pp. 131–140
6 teachings · 3 analogies · 5 one-liners

All spiritual endeavour is practice in remembering: nama japa keeps the name in memory, Self-enquiry keeps returning to 'Who am I?' Forgetfulness is not a sign of absorption but a fault - 'Forgetfulness is death.' If we have interest, love and awareness we will have memory; age affects only petty things. Awareness in the moment is what lets memory come when needed. The image: while Guruji speaks you hear only his voice; when he stops you hear the roar of the sea - the silence of the heart, happiness, bliss, love - always there in the background. Act fully, then be still; do not live like a cow ruminating grass.

Key teachings

Every spiritual practice is oriented to improving remembering.

The whole spiritual endeavour, the methods, whatever they may be, in different ways are all trying to improve our memory. They are practices of remembering.
p. 131

Nama japa is remembering: keeping the name in memory, not letting the mind forget it.

For instance, if you do nama japa, what are you doing? You are remembering, you are trying to keep the name in your memory, not letting the mind forget the name.
p. 132

Forgetfulness is death; poor memory is not a mark of spiritual absorption - 'That's bosh!'

Many people think, 'Oh, in spiritual life we forget about the mundane things because we are so absorbed in the Self,' but I don't agree. That's bosh!
p. 132

Interest, love and awareness give memory; age touches only petty things.

If we have the interest, if we have the love, if we have the awareness, we will also have the memory.
p. 132

The roar of the sea is always there: the silence of the heart, happiness, bliss, love - the background of all action.

When you are not listening to me, you listen to the roar of the sea - the silence of the heart, the happiness, the bliss, the love. The thrill of the love is like the roar of the sea.
p. 136

Experience fully in the moment; do not ruminate like a cow or buffalo.

Don't live like a buffalo or a cow! Live like human beings who eat thoroughly and experience fully in the moment.
p. 137

Analogies in this chapter

AnalogyWhat it describesGuruji's words (verbatim)
roar of the seaThis satsang was given on the coast near Chennai. While Guruji speaks you hear only his voice; when he stops you hear the sea's roar - always present, heard but not listened to. Awareness should be like that: act with full attention (listening to the voice), and in stillness hear the perpetual roar - the silence of the heart, bliss, love.I will tell you one thing. Now I am speaking to you and you are listening to my voice. When I am not speaking you hear the roar of the sea, or the breeze, or a dog barking, or someone coughing, but when I'm speaking you are not aware of those things. Your awareness should always be like that. (p. 136)
milk on the stove / catching fireTo boil milk or catch fire you must put it on the stove, on the flame - not in the refrigerator while the stove burns. Be here, be on the stove, to catch the fire of presence.In order to boil water or to heat milk, what do you do? You put it on the stove, on the fire, so that it catches the heat of the fire. If you really want to catch fire, be here and try to be on the stove! (p. 135)
cow / buffalo ruminatingA cow eats grass anxiously, afraid another creature will take it, then brings it back and ruminates. We experience the world the same way - anxiously, then ruminating alone afterwards instead of experiencing fully in the moment.What does a cow or a buffalo do? It eats avariciously because it is afraid it may not get the grass before another creature takes it, and then it slowly brings it back and tries to digest it. That is what we do, because of so many anxieties, frustrations and fears. (p. 137)

One-liners to remember

  1. If you are forgetting some things, you may also forget that you are with me! (p. 131)
  2. Forgetfulness is death. (p. 136)
  3. Let us be in complete awareness - of where we are, what we are doing, what we want to achieve, what we are getting and what we are not getting, and what we have to do in order to get that which we are not getting. (p. 136)
  4. Even if you forget, the only effort needed is to remember, because it is always there. You need not create the roar or this feeling - it is there. (p. 138)
  5. When you act, you act. When you are still, be still. (p. 138)

Arati Ch. 1 · January 2012

EN-001PictureArati

The photo plate of the January 2012 edition (Arati) is captioned with which place and year?

Answer: Bodhan, 2000The plate after the Arati chapter is captioned 'Bodhan, 2000' (the January monthly renders it 'Bodan, 2000').
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 14
Bodhan, 2000
EN-002AnalogyArati

Dasganu Maharaj said of himself, 'Apane masjida ka jharu Ganu hai.' What did he call himself - and what is a jharu?

Answer: A broom (jharu) in Baba's masjidThe broom is untouchable because it touches dirt, yet it alone collects the dust of Baba's feet and of devotees' feet, then rests untouched in a corner. Dasganu chose the lowest place as his devotion.
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 11
Dasganu Maharaj said, 'Apane masjida ka jharu Ganu hai' [Ganu is your mosque's broom].
EN-003AnalogyArati

Guruji compares choosing an arati to choosing what in a shop?

Answer: A greeting card to send best wishes to a friendIf you have no spontaneous expression of your own, pick a ready-made one that suits you - as you would pick a birthday card - and use it. Arati is such a ready-made expression: 'Send your best wishes to Baba!'
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 9
You go to a shop and search through all the different cards there until you find one you like, that you actually connect to. Then you buy that card and send it to your friend, on his birthday, or on Christmas, or on New Year's Day, whatever it is.
EN-004QuoteArati

In Baba's arati we sing 'Thaka gayi meri rasanaa.' What does Guruji say it means?

Answer: 'I am dumbfounded' (literally, 'my tongue is exhausted')Dasganu was expressing the stupefied feeling where one forgets oneself - the mind gone numb so it cannot speak. 'When we sing the arati our minds should get stupefied: that is the real arati.'
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 5
In Baba's arati we sing, 'Thaka gayi meri rasanaa,' that is, 'I am dumbfounded' [lit. 'my tongue is exhausted']. Actually, Dasganu Maharaj was expressing that stupefied feeling where one forgets oneself.
EN-005QuoteArati

Kabir said 'Main Ram ka kuttaa hun!' - 'I am Ram's dog!' What point does Guruji draw from it?

Answer: That egoless devotion means being happy with a single biscuit, always going around the master - unlike our ego, which bristles at bowing twice to Baba's photoKabir could have been Ram's friend, but chose to relate as his dog - 'even for only a biscuit' he goes wagging his tail, always around him. Guruji contrasts this with our problem even doing namaskar two or three times to Baba's photo.
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 11
Kabir said, 'Main Ram ka kuttaa hun!' 'I am Ram's dog!' That is what he compared himself to, and that is how he related himself to Ram, to his Baba.
EN-006ConceptArati

Which arati did Guruji call the most beautiful, and how did it begin?

Answer: Kakad Arati, Padh X, beginning 'Sai rahama nazara karanaa' (Lord Sai, look on us mercifully)Guruji liked Dasganu's aratis most, and of them this one above all - its attitude, diction, sentiment and author. He told satsangs they need sing only this arati if they wished.
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 5
Among the aratis, I like Dasganu's aratis most. Somehow, to me the most beautiful arati is the one that begins, 'Sai rahama nazara karanaa' [Lord Sai, look on us mercifully]. [Kakad Arati, Padh X]
EN-007ConceptArati

What does 'sathi akharakaa' mean, according to Guruji?

Answer: 'Companion from beginning to end' - Baba is there throughout, not just at the climaxNarasimha Swami translated it as 'last moment's companion', but Guruji corrects this: akharakaa means to the end, from beginning to end - not like the police in a Telugu movie who arrive in the last scene and are of no help.
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 8
Yes, sathi means 'companion' and akharakaa means 'to the end'. Actually, not only 'to the end' or 'at the climax', but 'from beginning to end' - he will be there.
EN-008MatchingArati

Match the chapter to its month: 'Arati' appeared in which 2012 monthly edition?

Answer: January 2012Arati is Chapter 1 of Rose Petals Vol. 2 and the January 2012 monthly edition (book pp. 3-14).
Source: Arati, January 2012, p. 3
Arati

The Aesthetic Experience Ch. 2 · February 2012

EN-009PictureThe Aesthetic Experience

The photo plate of the February 2012 edition (The Aesthetic Experience) is captioned with which place and year - and what is unusual about it?

Answer: Bodhan, 2000 - the same caption as the January 2012 photoFebruary's plate is captioned 'Bodhan, 2000' (monthly: 'Bodan, 2000'), exactly the same caption as January's. The sources do not say whether it is the same photograph reused or a different one from the same place and year.
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 24
Bodhan, 2000
EN-010AnalogyThe Aesthetic Experience

'I prefer that instead of going for the works of Michelangelo, everybody tries to be a Michelangelo.' What does Guruji mean?

Answer: That we should experience the art of our own mind and life, as Michelangelo experienced his own art in creating itEvery mind, every thought, every life is a piece of art - 'more beautiful than those by Michelangelo and Leonardo da Vinci'. The pieces of art outside us are only a reflection, a shadow, of our own self.
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 16
I prefer that instead of going for the works of Michelangelo, everybody tries to be a Michelangelo and experiences their own art.
EN-011AnalogyThe Aesthetic Experience

Guruji says 'all poetry is baaa baaas.' What is the analogy?

Answer: A poet tries to express the indescribable, and the longing behind the words is understood - as a mother understands each different cry of her baby, though there are no wordsThe rishis tried and tried to describe their experience and failed - that trying is poetry. 'A poet is one who tries.' Wordsworth's name itself is 'words-worth'.
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 21
In fact, all poetry is baaa baaas. The mother understands the longing behind it.
EN-012QuoteThe Aesthetic Experience

Complete the one-liner: 'That which affects the heart is ...'

Answer: real art!Scriptures are the rishis' ecstatic utterances - they sang and danced and those became the mantras. 'It is not science! I look at it as art.'
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 17
That which affects the heart is real art!
EN-013QuoteThe Aesthetic Experience

'This life is a novel, a beautiful novel! Written by whom? Written by Baba. He is the author.' What contrast does Guruji draw?

Answer: Read life like a novel to be enjoyed, not like a textbook to be annotated and reproduced under fear of failing'Remember your purpose! Then the book of life will be like a good novel, a pleasure to read; otherwise it will be like a textbook.' When we read a novel we remember, enjoy and can even reproduce it better.
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 18
This life is a novel, a beautiful novel! Written by whom? Written by Baba. He is the author. Try to read it and enjoy it - don't make it a textbook!
EN-014ConceptThe Aesthetic Experience

What is rasanubhuti (aesthetic experience), in Guruji's definition?

Answer: Feeling one with something, forgetting yourself - as when watching a movie you cry and laugh with the characters'Aesthetic experience is where you feel one with something, you forget yourself.' That is why even crying at a tragedy gives joy - it is an aesthetic experience, not sadism.
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 19
Aesthetic experience is where you feel one with something, you forget yourself. In Sanskrit it's called rasanubhuti, aesthetics.
EN-015ConceptThe Aesthetic Experience

Why did a man watch the Telugu tragedy 'Rakta Sambandham' two hundred times?

Answer: He went there just to cry - because even though crying is unpleasant, the aesthetic experience gives such joy'What is that? Crying is an unpleasant experience, so why does he crave it? Because even though he is crying, what he is getting there is an aesthetic experience, and that gives him such joy. That is the mystery of it!'
Source: The Aesthetic Experience, February 2012, p. 19
I know a person who has seen that movie two hundred times. Going there just to cry!
EN-016MatchingThe Aesthetic Experience

Match the chapter to its month: 'The Aesthetic Experience' appeared in which 2012 monthly edition?

Answer: February 2012The Aesthetic Experience is Chapter 2 of Rose Petals Vol. 2 and the February 2012 monthly edition (book pp. 15-24).

Start From Where You Are Ch. 3 · March 2012

EN-017PictureStart From Where You Are

The photo plate of the March 2012 edition (Start From Where You Are) is captioned with which place, occasion and year?

Answer: Shirdi, Vijayadasami 2004The book shortens the caption to 'Shirdi, 2004'; the March 2012 monthly edition gives the fuller caption 'Shirdi, Vijayadasami 2004'.
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 32
Shirdi, 2004
EN-018AnalogyStart From Where You Are

In the 'bird in the hand' analogy, what are the bird in the hand and the two birds in the bush?

Answer: The bird in hand is temporary happiness now; the two in the bush are the lasting happiness the scriptures promiseThose fixated on the bush lose even the happiness in hand. 'Let the path also be happy, not only the goal!' Temporary and lasting happiness are not two - 'whether it is in your hand or in the bush, in both cases they are birds!'
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 26
The small fulfilment you are getting now is like having one bird in the hand, and the lasting happiness which the scriptures promise, is like the proverbial 'two birds in the bush'!
EN-019AnalogyStart From Where You Are

What does Guruji mean by 'beating about the bush'?

Answer: Spending your whole life trying to achieve something you don't really want, instead of gaining even one percent from your real starting point'Start from there! Then everything you get - even if you get only one percent - is real! Otherwise, all your life you'll be beating about the bush, trying to achieve something you don't really want.'
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 27
Otherwise, all your life you'll be beating about the bush, trying to achieve something you don't really want.
EN-020QuoteStart From Where You Are

Complete Guruji's line: 'So let the path also be happy, ...'

Answer: not only the goal!'Why should the path to happiness be one of suffering? Let the path to happiness be also happy. Happily we enjoy, happily we dance, in a happy mood we move towards more and more happiness. We evolve in happiness, we evolve in fulfilment!'
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 26
So let the path also be happy, not only the goal! Why should the path to happiness be one of suffering?
EN-021QuoteStart From Where You Are

'But that nirvana should be your nirvana, your personal nirvana, not ...' - not whose?

Answer: Not the Buddha's nirvana, not Sai Baba's nirvana nor Ramana Maharshi's nirvana'Buddha got his nirvana, let me have my nirvana. He followed his path, let me follow my path.' That, Guruji says, is really following the Buddha - who said, 'Make your own path.'
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 29
But that nirvana should be your nirvana, your personal nirvana, not the Buddha's nirvana, not Sai Baba's nirvana nor Ramana Maharshi's nirvana - it should be your own nirvana!
EN-022ConceptStart From Where You Are

Why does Guruji call 'erasing desires' a dangerous concept?

Answer: Because it is not true for us - we have so many desires, and petty things like neck pain and headache will keep disturbing us while we talk of permanent happiness'It is not grounded in our reality... Even a small problem in our health will disturb us from trying for so-called lasting happiness.' Talking of 'killing the ego' while unable to sit for neck pain is 'all just tall talk'.'
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 27
It may be beautiful to listen to, but 'erasing desires' is another dangerous concept in the idea of lasting happiness because it is not true for us.
EN-023ConceptStart From Where You Are

What is the starting point of the search, according to Guruji?

Answer: Finding out what is missing - 'Something is missing' - and what exactly it is that you wantMany people have everything - food, security, bank balance, loving parents - yet feel something missing without knowing what. 'So this is the starting point for the search, the search for what is missing and how to fulfil it.'
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 28
So this is the starting point for the search, the search for what is missing and how to fulfil it.
EN-024MatchingStart From Where You Are

Match the chapter to its month: 'Start From Where You Are' appeared in which 2012 monthly edition?

Answer: March 2012Start From Where You Are is Chapter 3 of Rose Petals Vol. 2 and the March 2012 monthly edition (book pp. 25-32).
Source: Start From Where You Are, March 2012, p. 25
